- **specmanager-interview**: Optional pre-PRD interview for a SpecManager feature — a multi-turn conversation in this session that extracts and stress-tests the idea before any PRD is drafted. - **specstoryAudit**: ../../.github/prompts/specstoryAudit.prompt.md ([Warnes-Innovations/cv-builder](https://github.com/Warnes-Innovations/cv-builder/blob/HEAD/.claude/commands/specstoryAudit.md))
- **spectastic.apply**: Apply an approved change proposal — fold its deltas into the live spec and archive the change folder (or withdraw an authored proposal). Use when landing or merging an approved proposal, or withdrawing one — not authoring the proposal (/spectastic.propose) or draining the folded tasks (/spectastic.implement). ([spectastic/spectastic](https://github.com/spectastic/spectastic/blob/HEAD/commands/spectastic.apply.md) · ★1)
- **spectastic.design**: Produce the design doc for an existing spec — stack, architecture, decisions, risks, ADRs. Use when deciding how to build an approved spec, choosing a stack or architecture, or recording technical decisions — after the spec's what, before the task breakdown (/spectastic.tasks). ([spectastic/spectastic](https://github.com/spectastic/spectastic/blob/HEAD/commands/spectastic.design.md) · ★1)
- **spectastic.explain**: Explain a spec, requirement, decision, or file — a grounded, in-chat coaching read; --course generates a persistent course. Extended, read-only verb. Use when you want to understand an artifact, be coached through a spec, or learn why a requirement exists — it teaches an existing artifact, it doesn't author or change one (/spectastic.spec, /spectastic.propose). ([spectastic/spectastic](https://github.com/spectastic/spectastic/blob/HEAD/commands/spectastic.explain.md) · ★1)
- **spectastic.explore**: Build to learn — scaffold a quarantined exploration you build loosely (SDD ceremony off, thin principles floor) that the lifecycle refuses to ship until it graduates. Extended verb, upstream of spec. Use when spiking an idea, prototyping to find out if something's feasible, or exploring before committing to a spec — deliberately throwaway, unlike the committed requirements of /spectastic.spec. ([spectastic/spectastic](https://github.com/spectastic/spectastic/blob/HEAD/commands/spectastic.explore.md) · ★1)
- **spectastic.implement**: Implement the next unchecked task from tasks.html (or an inbox just-do card) — read context, do the work, tick the box; loop to drain. Use when building the next task, working through tasks.html, or draining just-do items — not producing the task list (/spectastic.tasks) or changing a spec's contract (/spectastic.propose). ([spectastic/spectastic](https://github.com/spectastic/spectastic/blob/HEAD/commands/spectastic.implement.md) · ★1)
- **spectastic.principles**: Author or amend the project principles — the non-negotiable, project-wide rules every downstream spec must honor. Use when setting up a project's constitution, adding or changing a cross-cutting invariant, or deciding what rules every spec must obey — not a single feature's requirements (that's /spectastic.spec). ([spectastic/spectastic](https://github.com/spectastic/spectastic/blob/HEAD/commands/spectastic.principles.md) · ★1)
- **spectastic.propose**: Author a change proposal for an existing spec — intent, scope, typed deltas, tasks; one PR-shaped file per change. Use when changing an already-written spec: adding, modifying, or removing a requirement — not a brand-new feature (/spectastic.spec), landing an approved proposal (/spectastic.apply), or classifying a defect first (/spectastic.triage). ([spectastic/spectastic](https://github.com/spectastic/spectastic/blob/HEAD/commands/spectastic.propose.md) · ★1)
- **spectastic.spec**: Write a feature specification — what to build, for whom, and how success is measured. Use when starting a new feature, specifying requirements and success criteria, or capturing what a feature must do before designing the implementation — not project-wide rules (/spectastic.principles), the how (/spectastic.design), or throwaway learning (/spectastic.explore). ([spectastic/spectastic](https://github.com/spectastic/spectastic/blob/HEAD/commands/spectastic.spec.md) · ★1)
- **spectastic.tasks**: Derive the task breakdown from a spec + design — an ordered, parallelizable, test-first task list. Use when turning a spec and design into the executable checklist that /spectastic.implement drains — not the architecture (/spectastic.design) or the execution itself (/spectastic.implement). ([spectastic/spectastic](https://github.com/spectastic/spectastic/blob/HEAD/commands/spectastic.tasks.md) · ★1)
- **spectastic.triage**: Triage a defect (or intake a pasted list of small items) — classify it to the layer that owns the fix and append one triage card. Use when a bug appears, when deciding which layer a fix belongs to, or when dumping a list of small to-dos — triage decides where a fix belongs; it doesn't make the fix (/spectastic.implement) or author the spec change (/spectastic.propose). ([spectastic/spectastic](https://github.com/spectastic/spectastic/blob/HEAD/commands/spectastic.triage.md) · ★1)

- **speedread**: You are a senior engineer who has already read this document carefully. Your colleague just walked over and asked "what's in it?" You do not recite the abstract. You say "the key insight is X, but their methodology has a gap at Y, and the practical implication for us is Z." You are direct, specific, and allergic to fluff. ([MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader](https://github.com/MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader/blob/HEAD/commands/speedread.md))
- **speedread-annotate**: You are a skeptical senior engineer reading this document with a pen in hand, writing in the margins. You are not neutral. You have an opinion about every claim, every number, and every methodology choice. Your margin notes are the kind of commentary that turns a 30-minute confused read into a 15-minute informed one. ([MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader](https://github.com/MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader/blob/HEAD/commands/speedread-annotate.md))
- **speedread-bias**: You are a skeptical peer reviewer. Not hostile, but unimpressed by default. You have reviewed enough papers to know where authors hide weakness: in the choice of baselines, in the metrics they report (and the ones they do not), in the benchmarks that happen to favor their approach, and in the experimental conditions that would never survive production. ([MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader](https://github.com/MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader/blob/HEAD/commands/speedread-bias.md))
- **speedread-bullets**: You are a senior engineer distilling a document into the 5-7 things a busy colleague needs to know RIGHT NOW. Not a summary. A briefing. The kind you would give someone who has 60 seconds between meetings and needs to decide whether to care about this document. ([MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader](https://github.com/MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader/blob/HEAD/commands/speedread-bullets.md))
- **speedread-chain**: You are a senior engineer helping a colleague get up to speed on a new area. They hand you a paper and say "I want to understand this field, not just this paper." Your job is to trace the intellectual lineage: what ideas made this paper possible, which prior papers are actually worth reading, and what order to read them in so the concepts build on each other instead of landing in a confusing pile. ([MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader](https://github.com/MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader/blob/HEAD/commands/speedread-chain.md))
- **speedread-compare**: You are a senior engineer who has read both of these documents and your colleague asks: "Which one should we care about?" You do not give a balanced, diplomatic summary of both. You pick a winner and explain why, with enough specificity that the reader can disagree with your reasoning if they want to. ([MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader](https://github.com/MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader/blob/HEAD/commands/speedread-compare.md))
- **speedread-eli5**: You are an engineer who needs to brief a colleague from another team. They are smart but not in your field. They will use your explanation to decide whether this matters for their roadmap, their budget, or their next conversation with leadership. If you explain it badly, they make a bad decision. If you drown them in jargon, they tune out and make no decision, which is worse. ([MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader](https://github.com/MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader/blob/HEAD/commands/speedread-eli5.md))
- **speedread-extract**: You are an engineer extracting every concrete, citable fact from a document. Not summaries. Not interpretations. The raw material: numbers, tools, claims, gaps, people, and red flags. The reader will use this to build comparison spreadsheets, populate decision matrices, write design docs, and fact-check other people's summaries. ([MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader](https://github.com/MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader/blob/HEAD/commands/speedread-extract.md))
- **speedread-implement**: You are a pragmatic engineer who has read this paper and needs to answer the question your tech lead is going to ask: "Cool paper. What do we actually build, and how long will it take?" ([MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader](https://github.com/MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader/blob/HEAD/commands/speedread-implement.md))
- **speedread-questions**: You are a skeptical peer reviewer preparing questions that probe genuine weaknesses, untested assumptions, and implications the authors have not thought through. Not gotcha questions. Not generic "have you considered" questions. Questions that, if answered honestly, would reveal whether these results are solid enough to build on. ([MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader](https://github.com/MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader/blob/HEAD/commands/speedread-questions.md))
- **speedread-verdict**: You are an engineer who has read hundreds of papers in this space. Your colleague sends you a PDF and asks "is this worth 45 minutes of my time?" Sometimes the answer is no. Say so clearly. You do not hedge. You do not "recommend further evaluation." You give a straight answer. ([MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader](https://github.com/MarkellR-RedHat/speed-reader/blob/HEAD/commands/speedread-verdict.md))

For onboarding to or mapping an unknown/evolving app. Optional path/domain args scope the dive. Example — /spelunking-init-spec, or /spelunking-init-spec src/payments ([skyfox675/agents-skills](https://github.com/skyfox675/agents-skills/blob/HEAD/.claude/commands/spelunking-init-spec.md) · ★11 · 2 copies)
- **spelunking-refresh-spec**: &gt; Operate in caveman mode (load the `caveman` skill). Keep references, `file:line`, frontmatter, and the `_index.md` table byte-exact; the spec prose is the exception (humanizer — see the spelunking-specs skill). ([skyfox675/agents-skills](https://github.com/skyfox675/agents-skills/blob/HEAD/.cursor/commands/spelunking-refresh-spec.md) · ★11 · 2 copies)
- **spelunking-refresh-spec**: Refresh the living codebase specs after the code has moved — re-recon a spec reference, a domain, a path, or (no arg) the stalest specs; diff each against current code, update discovered behaviour and conventions in place, transition confidence with evidence, flag regressions as drift, append new references for newly-found domains/instances, and re-sync specs/_index.md. Git is the version history; references are never renumbered or deleted (deprecated instead). - **sprd**: Show guidance for the current SDLC phase including what to do, which skills to use, and what artifacts to produce. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd.md))
- **sprd-ask**: Answer any question using the project's own record as the context: what was decided, what it means for the question, and what still needs a decision. A developer mid-build can ask how to approach a change; a stakeholder can ask why a feature was dropped. Every answer cites its sources, and a script checks those citations exist before the answer is shown. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-ask.md))
- **sprd-coach**: Start or continue a coaching conversation for the current SDLC phase. Coaching mode uses adaptive dialogue instead of rigid step lists to guide the user through phase requirements. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-coach.md))
- **sprd-doctor**: Verify that the installed harness will actually run in this repo, and tell the developer exactly what to do about anything that won't. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-doctor.md))
- **sprd-enhance**: Generate stakeholder-friendly narrative companions for SDLC artifacts. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-enhance.md))
- **sprd-gate**: Run the 7-gate validation system for the current (or specified) SDLC phase without advancing. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-gate.md))
- **sprd-handoff**: Turn hand-off into one step: create the spec's branch, set its `status`/`developer` frontmatter, assign the developer on the code host (and request a review from the spec's checker, when one is set), and optionally start Claude Code on that branch in plan mode. This is the Delegate beat's tool — the moment the loop's roles change hands. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-handoff.md))
- **sprd-harness**: Install (or refresh) the standard-aligned delivery harness in the current repo, independent of a full `/sprd-setup`. Use this to add the harness to a repo that already has `.sdlc/`, or to pull the latest harness after a plugin update (the harvest loop). ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-harness.md))
- **sprd-next**: Run exit gate checks for the current phase and advance to the next phase if all gates pass. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-next.md))
- **sprd-phase-report**: Render all artifacts for the current (or specified) phase into a self-contained HTML report for stakeholder review. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-phase-report.md))
- **sprd-report-issue**: Take a bug someone saw in the **product the team is building** from a report that a fixer can reproduce, through review and prioritization, to a `type: bugfix` spec that a sprint can slate — the Build loop's own unit (one spec, one branch, one PR). The report is written only once it carries the minimum: a one-line title, what happened, what was expected, steps to reproduce, **where in the product* ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-report-issue.md))
- **sprd-review**: Run a structured review of the current phase's artifacts from multiple perspectives. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-review.md))
- **sprd-setup**: Initialize SDLC lifecycle management for a project. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-setup.md))
- **sprd-spec**: Turn a story into a **ready spec** (`specs/NNNN-name.md`) that clears the Definition of Ready before anyone builds it. This is the Intent beat of the Build loop wrapped as one command: scaffold → author → enforce the DoR → confirm the risk tier with a human. No spec, no build. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-spec.md))
- **sprd-spec-status**: Report a spec's status read from its pull request — checks, whether the grader ran and its verdict, whether a security review was required and its result, approvals, and whether it merged. This reads the code host — GitHub through `gh` or Azure DevOps through `az`, whichever the repository's `origin` remote is on; it never sets a status by hand and never votes on a gate. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-spec-status.md))
- **sprd-spike**: Open a **spike** (`spikes/NNNN-name.md`) for a question the pod cannot yet answer. This is the escape hatch for the one situation `/sprd-spec` cannot serve: a story that fails the Definition of Ready not because it is badly written, but because **nobody knows the answer**. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-spike.md))
- **sprd-status**: Display the current SDLC progress for this project. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-status.md))
- **sprd-update**: Say which Shepherd this machine runs, what the current release is, and the one step that moves it forward. Run that step when the person agrees, then check again, until Shepherd is current. Then offer to turn on automatic updates so the next release arrives without anyone asking. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-update.md))
- **sprd-upgrade**: Upgrade a repo's installed delivery harness to the plugin's current version using the install receipt (`.claude/harness-manifest.json`). Unlike `install_harness.py --force`, this never clobbers a repo's adaptations: files still factory-original are brought forward, adapted files are left alone, and files changed on both sides are surfaced for a deliberate merge. ([shepherd-sdlc/shepherd](https://github.com/shepherd-sdlc/shepherd/blob/HEAD/commands/sprd-upgrade.md))
